========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten really… strange and inconsistent. For months now my appetite has been all over the place. There are stretches where I’ll just skip dinner, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because I can’t be bothered, or nothing sounds worth making, and then I realize my clothes are looser and I’ve lost weight without meaning to.

And then other nights I don’t really eat a meal either, I just sort of pick at crackers or cereal, standing in the kitchen, because cooking just for myself feels pointless. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been going on long enough that it started to worry me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything, but things that used to feel like they had some warmth to them just feel kind of… flat now.

Like my garden, for example. I used to really enjoy being out there, checking what was coming up, watering everything, noticing little changes. Now I’ll go outside and water because I know I should, but I’m not really in it. Sometimes I stand there with the hose and just feel like I’m doing a chore, and other times I don’t even get myself started.

And book club too. I still show up sometimes because I don’t want people asking questions, and because I used to love it, honestly. But I’ll sit there and have trouble caring about the discussion, even if it’s a book I would have been excited about before. A lot of the time I leave early, or I make an excuse not to go. It’s like I’m going through the motions of my own life, but the part of me that used to enjoy things isn’t really showing up.

I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I would call it “sad” exactly, at least not in the way I think of sadness, like sobbing or feeling devastated. It’s more like… a heaviness, or a dullness, I guess.

Most days I notice it’s there, but it kind of blends into the background, so I don’t always label it as being down. I can still get through conversations and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem fine. But internally, yes, there’s this low feeling that seems to hang around a lot of the day. Not always dramatic, just… present.

It’s been… longer than I want to admit, honestly. It really started around when my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which sounds so specific, but I only know because that date sort of marked a before-and-after in my head.

At first I thought it was just the stress of getting through everything, and I kept telling myself, you know, once the paperwork is done, once the dust settles, I’ll feel more like myself again. But then months kept passing, and the low feeling and not caring about things as much, and the eating being off… it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in some form since then.

  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… there have maybe been moments where I was distracted enough that I didn’t notice it as much, but I wouldn’t say it really went away for a few days at a time. It’s felt pretty consistently there since the divorce was finalized.

That was about a year and seven months ago now, and I keep coming back to that because it really feels like that’s when things shifted. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.

But then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.

So I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.

I still show up sometimes because I don’t want people asking questions, and because I used to love it, honestly. But I’ll sit there and have trouble caring about the discussion, even if it’s a book I would have been excited about before. A lot of the time I leave early, or I make an excuse not to go. It’s like I’m going through the motions of my own life, but the part of me that used to enjoy things isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, even if it's not intense enough to make you cry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I would call it “sad” exactly, at least not in the way I think of sadness, like sobbing or feeling devastated. It’s more like… a heaviness, or a dullness, I guess.\n\nMost days I notice it’s there, but it kind of blends into the background, so I don’t always label it as being down. I can still get through conversations and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem fine. But internally, yes, there’s this low feeling that seems to hang around a lot of the day. Not always dramatic, just… present.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the low mood, the lack of interest in things, and the changes in eating habits?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I want to admit, honestly. It really started around when my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which sounds so specific, but I only know because that date sort of marked a before-and-after in my head.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the stress of getting through everything, and I kept telling myself, you know, once the paperwork is done, once the dust settles, I’ll feel more like myself again. But then months kept passing, and the low feeling and not caring about things as much, and the eating being off… it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in some form since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when your mood felt a bit better, even for a few days, or has it been pretty consistently low since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… there have maybe been moments where I was distracted enough that I didn’t notice it as much, but I wouldn’t say it really went away for a few days at a time. It’s felt pretty consistently there since the divorce was finalized.\n\nThat was about a year and seven months ago now, and I keep coming back to that because it really feels like that’s when things shifted. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.

I keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.

Part of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.

And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.

The restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.

So… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.

It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.\n\nPart of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.\n\nThe restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have noticed that. It’s not that I’m collapsing or anything dramatic, but I feel like I’m moving through the day with this heaviness that wasn’t there before.

Even on days when my caseload at the hospital isn’t unusually bad — like, objectively, I can look at the schedule and say, “This is not an impossible day” — I still feel like I’m dragging my body from one place to another. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.

And then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. I’ll think, “I should just reply to her, it would take two minutes,” and then I just… don’t. Or I’ll stand in the kitchen and feel too tired to actually cook, even if I’m hungry.

So yes, less energy, definitely. It’s subtle in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it feels like everything costs more effort than it should.

It’s more like… a heaviness, or a dullness, I guess.\n\nMost days I notice it’s there, but it kind of blends into the background, so I don’t always label it as being down. I can still get through conversations and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem fine. But internally, yes, there’s this low feeling that seems to hang around a lot of the day. Not always dramatic, just… present.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the low mood, the lack of interest in things, and the changes in eating habits?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I want to admit, honestly. It really started around when my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which sounds so specific, but I only know because that date sort of marked a before-and-after in my head.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the stress of getting through everything, and I kept telling myself, you know, once the paperwork is done, once the dust settles, I’ll feel more like myself again. But then months kept passing, and the low feeling and not caring about things as much, and the eating being off… it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in some form since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when your mood felt a bit better, even for a few days, or has it been pretty consistently low since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… there have maybe been moments where I was distracted enough that I didn’t notice it as much, but I wouldn’t say it really went away for a few days at a time. It’s felt pretty consistently there since the divorce was finalized.\n\nThat was about a year and seven months ago now, and I keep coming back to that because it really feels like that’s when things shifted. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.\n\nPart of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.\n\nThe restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than before, or that even small tasks feel more exhausting than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that. It’s not that I’m collapsing or anything dramatic, but I feel like I’m moving through the day with this heaviness that wasn’t there before.\n\nEven on days when my caseload at the hospital isn’t unusually bad — like, objectively, I can look at the schedule and say, “This is not an impossible day” — I still feel like I’m dragging my body from one place to another. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. I’ll think, “I should just reply to her, it would take two minutes,” and then I just… don’t. Or I’ll stand in the kitchen and feel too tired to actually cook, even if I’m hungry.\n\nSo yes, less energy, definitely. It’s subtle in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it feels like everything costs more effort than it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F33.1", "F33.2", "F33.3", "F33.8", "F33.9"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in a way other people would notice, at least.

I mean, I do feel like it takes effort to get through things, but that feels more like energy than like my thoughts are actually slowed down. In conversations at work, I’m still responding, still following what people are saying, still able to do what I need to do. I haven’t had anyone say, “You seem slow,” or “Are you okay, you’re taking a long time to answer?” Nothing like that.

Sometimes when I’m tired, I might pause more or feel like I have to gather myself before speaking, but I can’t honestly say it’s some major noticeable slowing. And physically, I don’t think I’m moving unusually slowly. I’m probably less efficient at home, but that’s not the same thing as other people seeing me move in slow motion or anything.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed that specifically. It’s more that everything feels harder internally, but I don’t think it’s showing as slowed speech or movement.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten really… strange and inconsistent. For months now my appetite has been all over the place. There are stretches where I’ll just skip dinner, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because I can’t be bothered, or nothing sounds worth making, and then I realize my clothes are looser and I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I don’t really eat a meal either, I just sort of pick at crackers or cereal, standing in the kitchen, because cooking just for myself feels pointless. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been going on long enough that it started to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything, but things that used to feel like they had some warmth to them just feel kind of… flat now.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really enjoy being out there, checking what was coming up, watering everything, noticing little changes. Now I’ll go outside and water because I know I should, but I’m not really in it. Sometimes I stand there with the hose and just feel like I’m doing a chore, and other times I don’t even get myself started.\n\nAnd book club too. I still show up sometimes because I don’t want people asking questions, and because I used to love it, honestly. But I’ll sit there and have trouble caring about the discussion, even if it’s a book I would have been excited about before. A lot of the time I leave early, or I make an excuse not to go. It’s like I’m going through the motions of my own life, but the part of me that used to enjoy things isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, even if it's not intense enough to make you cry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I would call it “sad” exactly, at least not in the way I think of sadness, like sobbing or feeling devastated. It’s more like… a heaviness, or a dullness, I guess.\n\nMost days I notice it’s there, but it kind of blends into the background, so I don’t always label it as being down. I can still get through conversations and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem fine. But internally, yes, there’s this low feeling that seems to hang around a lot of the day. Not always dramatic, just… present.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the low mood, the lack of interest in things, and the changes in eating habits?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I want to admit, honestly. It really started around when my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which sounds so specific, but I only know because that date sort of marked a before-and-after in my head.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the stress of getting through everything, and I kept telling myself, you know, once the paperwork is done, once the dust settles, I’ll feel more like myself again. But then months kept passing, and the low feeling and not caring about things as much, and the eating being off… it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in some form since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when your mood felt a bit better, even for a few days, or has it been pretty consistently low since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… there have maybe been moments where I was distracted enough that I didn’t notice it as much, but I wouldn’t say it really went away for a few days at a time. It’s felt pretty consistently there since the divorce was finalized.\n\nThat was about a year and seven months ago now, and I keep coming back to that because it really feels like that’s when things shifted. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.\n\nPart of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.\n\nThe restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than before, or that even small tasks feel more exhausting than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that. It’s not that I’m collapsing or anything dramatic, but I feel like I’m moving through the day with this heaviness that wasn’t there before.\n\nEven on days when my caseload at the hospital isn’t unusually bad — like, objectively, I can look at the schedule and say, “This is not an impossible day” — I still feel like I’m dragging my body from one place to another. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. I’ll think, “I should just reply to her, it would take two minutes,” and then I just… don’t. Or I’ll stand in the kitchen and feel too tired to actually cook, even if I’m hungry.\n\nSo yes, less energy, definitely. It’s subtle in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it feels like everything costs more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to get yourself moving, in a way that other people might notice, like taking longer to respond during conversations or moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way other people would notice, at least.\n\nI mean, I do feel like it takes effort to get through things, but that feels more like energy than like my thoughts are actually slowed down. In conversations at work, I’m still responding, still following what people are saying, still able to do what I need to do. I haven’t had anyone say, “You seem slow,” or “Are you okay, you’re taking a long time to answer?” Nothing like that.\n\nSometimes when I’m tired, I might pause more or feel like I have to gather myself before speaking, but I can’t honestly say it’s some major noticeable slowing. And physically, I don’t think I’m moving unusually slowly. I’m probably less efficient at home, but that’s not the same thing as other people seeing me move in slow motion or anything.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that specifically. It’s more that everything feels harder internally, but I don’t think it’s showing as slowed speech or movement.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. I’ll think, “I should just reply to her, it would take two minutes,” and then I just… don’t. Or I’ll stand in the kitchen and feel too tired to actually cook, even if I’m hungry.\n\nSo yes, less energy, definitely. It’s subtle in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it feels like everything costs more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to get yourself moving, in a way that other people might notice, like taking longer to respond during conversations or moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way other people would notice, at least.\n\nI mean, I do feel like it takes effort to get through things, but that feels more like energy than like my thoughts are actually slowed down. In conversations at work, I’m still responding, still following what people are saying, still able to do what I need to do. I haven’t had anyone say, “You seem slow,” or “Are you okay, you’re taking a long time to answer?” Nothing like that.\n\nSometimes when I’m tired, I might pause more or feel like I have to gather myself before speaking, but I can’t honestly say it’s some major noticeable slowing. And physically, I don’t think I’m moving unusually slowly. I’m probably less efficient at home, but that’s not the same thing as other people seeing me move in slow motion or anything.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that specifically. It’s more that everything feels harder internally, but I don’t think it’s showing as slowed speech or movement.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had episodes like this before, where your mood was low and you lost interest in things for weeks or more, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for an extended period?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not completely sure how to answer that.

I don’t think I can point to a clear prior episode like this and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.” I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches before — I think most people have, and working in healthcare, there are definitely periods where you feel worn down or emotionally flat for a bit. But in terms of low mood and losing interest in things for weeks or more… I don’t know. I’m not aware of a distinct pattern like that.

This feels more extended than what I would normally just call a bad week or being stressed. But I’m hesitant to say this is definitely the first time ever, because memory gets kind of blurry when you’re looking back over years. I can remember times when I was unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t know that I lost interest in things in this same way or that it went on like this.

So I guess the most accurate answer is: I don’t know of previous episodes like this clearly. This is the first time I’ve really recognized it as something that has lasted and felt hard to shake.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten really… strange and inconsistent. For months now my appetite has been all over the place. There are stretches where I’ll just skip dinner, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because I can’t be bothered, or nothing sounds worth making, and then I realize my clothes are looser and I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I don’t really eat a meal either, I just sort of pick at crackers or cereal, standing in the kitchen, because cooking just for myself feels pointless. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been going on long enough that it started to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything, but things that used to feel like they had some warmth to them just feel kind of… flat now.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really enjoy being out there, checking what was coming up, watering everything, noticing little changes. Now I’ll go outside and water because I know I should, but I’m not really in it. Sometimes I stand there with the hose and just feel like I’m doing a chore, and other times I don’t even get myself started.\n\nAnd book club too. I still show up sometimes because I don’t want people asking questions, and because I used to love it, honestly. But I’ll sit there and have trouble caring about the discussion, even if it’s a book I would have been excited about before. A lot of the time I leave early, or I make an excuse not to go. It’s like I’m going through the motions of my own life, but the part of me that used to enjoy things isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, even if it's not intense enough to make you cry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I would call it “sad” exactly, at least not in the way I think of sadness, like sobbing or feeling devastated. It’s more like… a heaviness, or a dullness, I guess.\n\nMost days I notice it’s there, but it kind of blends into the background, so I don’t always label it as being down. I can still get through conversations and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem fine. But internally, yes, there’s this low feeling that seems to hang around a lot of the day. Not always dramatic, just… present.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the low mood, the lack of interest in things, and the changes in eating habits?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I want to admit, honestly. It really started around when my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which sounds so specific, but I only know because that date sort of marked a before-and-after in my head.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the stress of getting through everything, and I kept telling myself, you know, once the paperwork is done, once the dust settles, I’ll feel more like myself again. But then months kept passing, and the low feeling and not caring about things as much, and the eating being off… it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in some form since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when your mood felt a bit better, even for a few days, or has it been pretty consistently low since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… there have maybe been moments where I was distracted enough that I didn’t notice it as much, but I wouldn’t say it really went away for a few days at a time. It’s felt pretty consistently there since the divorce was finalized.\n\nThat was about a year and seven months ago now, and I keep coming back to that because it really feels like that’s when things shifted. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.\n\nPart of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.\n\nThe restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than before, or that even small tasks feel more exhausting than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that. It’s not that I’m collapsing or anything dramatic, but I feel like I’m moving through the day with this heaviness that wasn’t there before.\n\nEven on days when my caseload at the hospital isn’t unusually bad — like, objectively, I can look at the schedule and say, “This is not an impossible day” — I still feel like I’m dragging my body from one place to another. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. I’ll think, “I should just reply to her, it would take two minutes,” and then I just… don’t. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than before, or that even small tasks feel more exhausting than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that. It’s not that I’m collapsing or anything dramatic, but I feel like I’m moving through the day with this heaviness that wasn’t there before.\n\nEven on days when my caseload at the hospital isn’t unusually bad — like, objectively, I can look at the schedule and say, “This is not an impossible day” — I still feel like I’m dragging my body from one place to another. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. No… I don’t think so. Not really.

I mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been busy or running on adrenaline, like when work is demanding and you just sort of push through because things need to get done. But that doesn’t feel like my mood was unusually high or like I suddenly had all this energy in a good way. It’s more just… necessity, I guess.

I’m generally pretty controlled — maybe too controlled — so if anything, I tend to overthink things rather than suddenly do things without considering them.

It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s subtle in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it feels like everything costs more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to get yourself moving, in a way that other people might notice, like taking longer to respond during conversations or moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way other people would notice, at least.\n\nI mean, I do feel like it takes effort to get through things, but that feels more like energy than like my thoughts are actually slowed down. In conversations at work, I’m still responding, still following what people are saying, still able to do what I need to do. I haven’t had anyone say, “You seem slow,” or “Are you okay, you’re taking a long time to answer?” Nothing like that.\n\nSometimes when I’m tired, I might pause more or feel like I have to gather myself before speaking, but I can’t honestly say it’s some major noticeable slowing. And physically, I don’t think I’m moving unusually slowly. I’m probably less efficient at home, but that’s not the same thing as other people seeing me move in slow motion or anything.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that specifically. It’s more that everything feels harder internally, but I don’t think it’s showing as slowed speech or movement.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes like this before, where your mood was low and you lost interest in things for weeks or more, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure how to answer that.\n\nI don’t think I can point to a clear prior episode like this and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.” I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches before — I think most people have, and working in healthcare, there are definitely periods where you feel worn down or emotionally flat for a bit. But in terms of low mood and losing interest in things for weeks or more… I don’t know. I’m not aware of a distinct pattern like that.\n\nThis feels more extended than what I would normally just call a bad week or being stressed. But I’m hesitant to say this is definitely the first time ever, because memory gets kind of blurry when you’re looking back over years. I can remember times when I was unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t know that I lost interest in things in this same way or that it went on like this.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: I don’t know of previous episodes like this clearly. This is the first time I’ve really recognized it as something that has lasted and felt hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your mood was unusually high, you felt extremely energetic, or you were more impulsive than usual — even for a few days?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been busy or running on adrenaline, like when work is demanding and you just sort of push through because things need to get done. But that doesn’t feel like my mood was unusually high or like I suddenly had all this energy in a good way. It’s more just… necessity, I guess.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt euphoric or like I didn’t need to slow down. And I don’t think I’ve been more impulsive than usual in any clear way. I’m generally pretty controlled — maybe too controlled — so if anything, I tend to overthink things rather than suddenly do things without considering them.\n\nSo, no, I can’t think of anything like that, even for a few days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can say yes to that clearly.

I’ve definitely had times in my life where I was more stressed or sad or kind of worn down, and maybe for more than a few days. But when you say two weeks or more, most of the day, with losing interest in things or those other changes… I don’t know that I can identify a specific period like that before.

Part of me wants to say maybe, just because life has had hard stretches and I don’t always remember exactly how long things lasted. But I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than it is. I can remember being overwhelmed, or not feeling like myself, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing or if it met that threshold.

So I guess my answer is… I don’t really know. I can’t point to a prior two-week period and say, yes, that definitely happened.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is my eating has just gotten really… strange and inconsistent. For months now my appetite has been all over the place. There are stretches where I’ll just skip dinner, not because I’m trying to diet or anything, but because I can’t be bothered, or nothing sounds worth making, and then I realize my clothes are looser and I’ve lost weight without meaning to.\n\nAnd then other nights I don’t really eat a meal either, I just sort of pick at crackers or cereal, standing in the kitchen, because cooking just for myself feels pointless. It sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s been going on long enough that it started to worry me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything, but things that used to feel like they had some warmth to them just feel kind of… flat now.\n\nLike my garden, for example. I used to really enjoy being out there, checking what was coming up, watering everything, noticing little changes. Now I’ll go outside and water because I know I should, but I’m not really in it. Sometimes I stand there with the hose and just feel like I’m doing a chore, and other times I don’t even get myself started.\n\nAnd book club too. I still show up sometimes because I don’t want people asking questions, and because I used to love it, honestly. But I’ll sit there and have trouble caring about the discussion, even if it’s a book I would have been excited about before. A lot of the time I leave early, or I make an excuse not to go. It’s like I’m going through the motions of my own life, but the part of me that used to enjoy things isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, even if it's not intense enough to make you cry?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I would call it “sad” exactly, at least not in the way I think of sadness, like sobbing or feeling devastated. It’s more like… a heaviness, or a dullness, I guess.\n\nMost days I notice it’s there, but it kind of blends into the background, so I don’t always label it as being down. I can still get through conversations and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside I probably seem fine. But internally, yes, there’s this low feeling that seems to hang around a lot of the day. Not always dramatic, just… present.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way — the low mood, the lack of interest in things, and the changes in eating habits?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I want to admit, honestly. It really started around when my divorce was finalized. That was about a year and seven months ago now, which sounds so specific, but I only know because that date sort of marked a before-and-after in my head.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the stress of getting through everything, and I kept telling myself, you know, once the paperwork is done, once the dust settles, I’ll feel more like myself again. But then months kept passing, and the low feeling and not caring about things as much, and the eating being off… it didn’t really lift. It’s been there in some form since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when your mood felt a bit better, even for a few days, or has it been pretty consistently low since this started?\n\nPatient: I guess… there have maybe been moments where I was distracted enough that I didn’t notice it as much, but I wouldn’t say it really went away for a few days at a time. It’s felt pretty consistently there since the divorce was finalized.\n\nThat was about a year and seven months ago now, and I keep coming back to that because it really feels like that’s when things shifted. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.\n\nPart of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.\n\nThe restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. It’s more internal than visible, I think.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than before, or that even small tasks feel more exhausting than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have noticed that. It’s not that I’m collapsing or anything dramatic, but I feel like I’m moving through the day with this heaviness that wasn’t there before.\n\nEven on days when my caseload at the hospital isn’t unusually bad — like, objectively, I can look at the schedule and say, “This is not an impossible day” — I still feel like I’m dragging my body from one place to another. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left. Things that should be small — making something decent to eat, stretching for ten minutes, answering a couple of personal messages — they feel like these huge tasks. I’ll think, “I should just reply to her, it would take two minutes,” and then I just… don’t. Or I’ll stand in the kitchen and feel too tired to actually cook, even if I’m hungry.\n\nSo yes, less energy, definitely. It’s subtle in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it feels like everything costs more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts feel slowed down or that it's harder to get yourself moving, in a way that other people might notice, like taking longer to respond during conversations or moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way other people would notice, at least.\n\nI mean, I do feel like it takes effort to get through things, but that feels more like energy than like my thoughts are actually slowed down. In conversations at work, I’m still responding, still following what people are saying, still able to do what I need to do. I haven’t had anyone say, “You seem slow,” or “Are you okay, you’re taking a long time to answer?” Nothing like that.\n\nSometimes when I’m tired, I might pause more or feel like I have to gather myself before speaking, but I can’t honestly say it’s some major noticeable slowing. And physically, I don’t think I’m moving unusually slowly. I’m probably less efficient at home, but that’s not the same thing as other people seeing me move in slow motion or anything.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that specifically. It’s more that everything feels harder internally, but I don’t think it’s showing as slowed speech or movement.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had episodes like this before, where your mood was low and you lost interest in things for weeks or more, or is this the first time you’ve felt this way for an extended period?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure how to answer that.\n\nI don’t think I can point to a clear prior episode like this and say, “Yes, that was the same thing.” I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches before — I think most people have, and working in healthcare, there are definitely periods where you feel worn down or emotionally flat for a bit. But in terms of low mood and losing interest in things for weeks or more… I don’t know. I’m not aware of a distinct pattern like that.\n\nThis feels more extended than what I would normally just call a bad week or being stressed. But I’m hesitant to say this is definitely the first time ever, because memory gets kind of blurry when you’re looking back over years. I can remember times when I was unhappy or overwhelmed, but I don’t know that I lost interest in things in this same way or that it went on like this.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: I don’t know of previous episodes like this clearly. This is the first time I’ve really recognized it as something that has lasted and felt hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where your mood was unusually high, you felt extremely energetic, or you were more impulsive than usual — even for a few days?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not really.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I’ve been busy or running on adrenaline, like when work is demanding and you just sort of push through because things need to get done. But that doesn’t feel like my mood was unusually high or like I suddenly had all this energy in a good way. It’s more just… necessity, I guess.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt euphoric or like I didn’t need to slow down. And I don’t think I’ve been more impulsive than usual in any clear way. I’m generally pretty controlled — maybe too controlled — so if anything, I tend to overthink things rather than suddenly do things without considering them.\n\nSo, no, I can’t think of anything like that, even for a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting two weeks or more when you felt down most of the day, lost interest in things, or had other symptoms like changes in sleep or appetite — even if it wasn’t quite as long or as severe as what you’re describing now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say yes to that clearly.\n\nI’ve definitely had times in my life where I was more stressed or sad or kind of worn down, and maybe for more than a few days. But when you say two weeks or more, most of the day, with losing interest in things or those other changes… I don’t know that I can identify a specific period like that before.\n\nPart of me wants to say maybe, just because life has had hard stretches and I don’t always remember exactly how long things lasted. But I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than it is. I can remember being overwhelmed, or not feeling like myself, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing or if it met that threshold.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t really know. I can’t point to a prior two-week period and say, yes, that definitely happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, in the sense that the loss of interest part is definitely there now. I’m still technically doing some of the things I used to care about, but it feels like I’m sort of acting out the motions rather than actually enjoying them.

Like, I’ll go outside to water the garden because I know the plants need it, and normally that would be something I’d take pleasure in — checking what’s blooming, fussing with things, noticing little changes. Lately I just stand there and do the bare minimum, or I look at it and think, “I should go out,” and then I don’t even start. And book club is similar. I’ll show up because I said I would, and because not going feels like it would be noticeable, but I’m not really engaged. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe say a little bit, and then I leave early if I can.

So… it’s not that I’ve stopped everything completely. That’s partly why I kept wondering if it “counts,” because from the outside I probably still look functional. But inside, most of those things feel flat. They don’t pull me in the way they used to.

Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I’m probably less efficient at home, but that’s not the same thing as other people seeing me move in slow motion or anything.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that specifically. But when you say two weeks or more, most of the day, with losing interest in things or those other changes… I don’t know that I can identify a specific period like that before.\n\nPart of me wants to say maybe, just because life has had hard stretches and I don’t always remember exactly how long things lasted. But I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds more definite than it is. I can remember being overwhelmed, or not feeling like myself, but I don’t know if it was the same kind of thing or if it met that threshold.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I don’t really know. I can’t point to a prior two-week period and say, yes, that definitely happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt down most of the day, nearly every day, and lost interest in most things, lasting at least two weeks — even if it wasn’t as long or severe as what you’re describing now?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, in the sense that the loss of interest part is definitely there now. I’m still technically doing some of the things I used to care about, but it feels like I’m sort of acting out the motions rather than actually enjoying them.\n\nLike, I’ll go outside to water the garden because I know the plants need it, and normally that would be something I’d take pleasure in — checking what’s blooming, fussing with things, noticing little changes. Lately I just stand there and do the bare minimum, or I look at it and think, “I should go out,” and then I don’t even start. And book club is similar. I’ll show up because I said I would, and because not going feels like it would be noticeable, but I’m not really engaged. I’ll sit there and listen, maybe say a little bit, and then I leave early if I can.\n\nSo… it’s not that I’ve stopped everything completely. That’s partly why I kept wondering if it “counts,” because from the outside I probably still look functional. But inside, most of those things feel flat. I kept waiting for it to ease up after everything was settled, like maybe I just needed time, but it’s sort of stayed in the background since then. Not always at the exact same level every single hour, but consistently low enough that I can’t honestly say there have been real stretches where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep — like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping much more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has definitely changed, though it’s not so much that I can’t get into bed or that I’m up all night from the start. I usually do get into bed at a fairly reasonable time, and sometimes I even fall asleep okay, which almost makes it feel like it shouldn’t count as a sleep problem.\n\nBut then I’ll wake up around four in the morning — it’s usually somewhere around then — and my brain just starts going. It’ll be bills, or something I need to finish for work, or notes I didn’t complete, or sometimes things related to the divorce that I thought I was done thinking about. And once I’m awake, I’m just… awake. I’ll lie there feeling exhausted, knowing the alarm is going to go off soon, and I can’t really get back to proper sleep.\n\nSo I’m not necessarily sleeping much more or going to bed extremely late. It’s more that the sleep I get feels interrupted, and those early mornings are hard because I’m tired but my mind won’t shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or blaming yourself for things more often than usual since the divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I probably say out loud.\n\nI keep going back over the marriage in my head, almost like I’m reviewing a case file, except it’s my own life and there’s no objective distance. I’ll think, “If I had been more patient,” or “If I had noticed things sooner,” or “If I had handled certain conversations differently, maybe it wouldn’t have ended this way.” And then it turns into this larger thought that I must have failed at something really basic — being a partner, keeping a family or a life together, just… something fundamental.\n\nPart of me knows relationships are more complicated than that. I do know that, intellectually. I would say that to a client without hesitation. But when it’s me, it doesn’t land the same way. I still find myself blaming myself, or feeling like the divorce says something about my worth or my ability to get important things right. It’s not constant every second, but it comes up a lot, especially when I’m alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or feeling restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in a clear way. I mean, I don’t think anyone has said to me that I’m moving slowly, and I haven’t really had that sense of being physically slowed down like I can’t get across a room or anything like that.\n\nThe restless part is harder to answer, because when I’m alone with my thoughts I can feel very unsettled internally, like my mind is pacing, if that makes sense. But I don’t know that my body is actually restless in the way you’re asking — like I’m not constantly getting up and walking around, or unable to sit in a chair. It’s more that I’m sitting there and everything inside feels noisy.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change in how I move, at least not one I could confidently describe. I’ll get through it because I have to, and I can still do the work, but it takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nAnd then when I get home, it’s like there’s just nothing left.
